I read the brief (`docs/TASKS.md`) and the seed list (`docs/ourcrowd_companies.txt`, 258 companies, names only). The repo has no `CONTEXT.md` and no ADRs yet. I'll create them once we settle the first terms.

Some things I noticed in the data that will come up later:
- **Only 12 entries carry a hint**, either a domain (`Lambda (lambda.ai)`) or a former name (`Lifeward (formerly known as ReWalk)`). Every other entry is a bare name.
- **Many names are ordinary words**: Harvey, Wave, Ro, Peak, Near, Shield, Guild, Astra, Silo, Orchard, Launchpad, Casper, Tala, Bites, Island, Overtime. A plain keyword search for these will mostly return unrelated articles.
- **Some companies are huge**: SpaceX, Stripe, Anthropic, xAI and Databricks will each get thousands of articles a quarter. Many small or defunct ones (Magisto, Nanorep, BillGuard) will get none.

**My assumption, which you can veto:** the brief requires "JavaScript (Node.js)" and the repo template is NestJS + TypeScript. TypeScript compiles to and runs on Node, so I'm treating it as allowed and will record that in the README's assumptions.

---

### Q1: Where does the news come from?

Everything else depends on this: how far back we can see, what links we can store, rate limits, and whether reviewers need an API key.

| Option | Covers last 90 days? | Key needed? | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Google News RSS search** (`news.google.com/rss/search?q="X"+when:90d`) | Yes | No | Free, broad coverage. Returns at most about 100 items per query. Links are Google redirect URLs that need resolving to reach the real article. Not an official API, so the format could change. |
| **GDELT DOC 2.0 API** | Yes (rolling 3 months) | No | Free, official and gives the real article URLs. Returns at most 250 items per query. Results are noisy and weighted toward non-English sources. Gives titles only, with no article snippet. |
| NewsAPI.org | No: the free tier only goes back 1 month and is licensed for development use only | Yes | Doesn't meet the quarterly requirement. |
| Scraping each company's own press page | Partial | No | That's the company's own announcements, not press coverage. |

**My recommendation:** use Google News RSS as the one real source and put it behind a `NewsSource` interface (the codebase calls these "ports"). That way GDELT could be added later as a second source without changing any calling code. It's the only option that covers the full quarter, needs no key (so a reviewer can run it with nothing but Ollama installed), and returns a snippet the LLM can classify. The README would name its limitations: the roughly 100-item cap per query, the format being unofficial, and the redirect links.

Do you agree, or do you already have a source in mind, or a paid key you want to use?

### Assistant [2026-10-07T06:36:21.386Z]
I've created `CONTEXT.md` with **Tracked Company** and **News Source**. The extensibility requirement is noted. Because the interface is named for what callers need ("find news items about this company in this time window") and not for how Google works, adding a NewsAPI source or a scraper later just means a new file under `repositories/` and a new provider binding. No calling code changes.

---

### Q2: When does a news item count as a Mention?

The News Source returns **candidates**. Searching `"Harvey"` gives you Harvey Weinstein and Hurricane Harvey. `"Wave"` and `"Ro"` are worse. Even `"Island"` (the enterprise browser company) is drowned out by travel news. If we store everything the search returns, the dashboard is wrong for at least 30–40 of the 258 companies.

The question splits into two parts:

**(a) Where does the disambiguating context come from?** The seed list is names only. I recommend we keep `ourcrowd_companies.txt` as the source of truth for *who* is tracked, and add a curated `companies.json` next to it that enriches each entry with optional `aliases` (including the "formerly X" names), `domain`, a one-line `description`/sector, and `searchTerms`. Only the ambiguous names need search terms. Example: Harvey becomes `"Harvey" AI legal`. An entry with no enrichment falls back to its quoted name. A startup check fails loudly if `companies.json` names a company that isn't in the seed list.

**(b) Who makes the final call?** I recommend that Ollama decides relevance in the **same call** as sentiment. The prompt gets the company's name, aliases and description plus the article title and snippet. It returns `{ relevant: boolean, sentiment, reason }`. An irrelevant item is **kept but marked rejected**, not dropped. That gives an audit trail for the "how did you validate quality" section of the README. It also stops the daily job from re-classifying the same irrelevant article every day.

So, proposed definitions:
- **Candidate**: any item a News Source returns for a Tracked Company.
- **Mention**: a candidate the classifier confirmed is about that Tracked Company. Only Mentions appear on the dashboard, count toward the "last mentioned" status, and trigger alerts.

Do you agree with both parts? In particular, are you OK with hand-curating search terms for the ambiguous names, or do you want something more automatic, such as having the LLM generate the descriptions once?

### Assistant [2026-10-07T06:52:11.764Z]
Both checks happen **per article**, and each article is classified **exactly once**. I think two different things got mixed together in my last message:

| What | How often | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| **Search disambiguation** | Once per company, curated by hand | `"Harvey" AI legal` instead of `"Harvey"`. This narrows what the News Source returns. It's config, not an LLM call. |
| **Relevance** | Once per article | "Is *this* article about Harvey the legal-AI company?" Even with good search terms, some results will still be about Harvey Weinstein. |
| **Sentiment** | Once per article, Mentions only | "Is this article positive, negative or neutral *toward the company*?" |

Neither check is ever repeated. Articles are deduplicated by URL, so the daily job only classifies articles it hasn't seen before.

So the real question is **one Ollama call per article or two**. Thinking it through again, I'm changing my recommendation: **use two separate steps.**

- **Sentiment only runs on Mentions.** For ambiguous companies, a lot of candidates get rejected. Making relevance a separate first step skips sentiment entirely for those, which recovers most of what I hoped to save by combining the calls.
- **Each is easier to check on its own.** With two prompts we can spot-check the relevance step's precision separately from sentiment accuracy, and the README's validation section can report both.
- **Single responsibility.** A `RelevanceClassifier` and a `SentimentClassifier`, each behind its own interface, can be changed, swapped to a different model, or tested independently. A combined prompt that answers two questions at once is harder to tune, because improving one answer tends to change the other.
- **The cost is small.** It's one extra short call per relevant article, and backfill is a one-time run.

The relevance verdict is stored on the article with a short `reason`, so rejected items stay visible for auditing.

**Revised proposal:** Candidate → `RelevanceClassifier` (once per article) → if relevant, it becomes a **Mention** → `SentimentClassifier` (once per Mention). Search terms stay as per-company config in `companies.json`.

Does that match what you had in mind? And are you OK with hand-curating `companies.json` (aliases, description, and search terms for the ambiguous names)?

### Q3: What exactly is "the last quarter"?

Today is 2026-10-07, so the brief's "last quarter" has three plausible readings:

| Reading | Window today | Behaviour over time |
|---|---|---|
| **Rolling 90 days** | 2026-07-09 → today | Always current. Every daily run adds new days and drops old ones. |
| **Previous calendar quarter** | Q3: 2026-07-01 → 2026-09-30 | A fixed report. It ignores everything since 1 October and stays frozen until January. |
| **Quarter to date** | 2026-10-01 → today | Only 7 days of data right now, so it looks almost empty for weeks after each quarter starts. |

**My recommendation:** use a rolling 90-day window by default, with a quarter selector that switches to fixed calendar quarters (Q3 2026, Q4-to-date). The daily alert job and the "last mentioned" status are about *now*, and a dashboard frozen at 30 September would contradict them. Investor-relations people do think in calendar quarters, though, and the selector costs little because we store each Mention's publication date anyway.

One consequence: the first run is a **backfill**, a one-time fetch of the previous ~90+ days, kept separate from the daily **incremental** check. The backfill must not fire 258 companies' worth of alerts. I'll ask about that separately.

Rolling 90 days plus a calendar-quarter selector, or do you read "last quarter" differently?

### Q4: What exactly triggers an alert, and where does it go?

There are four sub-decisions. Here's my recommendation for each.

**(a) What does "new" mean?** A Mention is new if it was **first seen** in this Daily Check. Its publication date doesn't decide that. Google News often surfaces an article a day or two after it's published, and a rule based on publication date would miss those. There is one guard: a Mention first seen today but published more than **7 days ago** is stored and shown on the dashboard, but it doesn't trigger an alert, because by then it isn't news.

**(b) What about the Backfill?** It **never alerts**. Otherwise the first run would send thousands of "new" Mentions.

**(c) One alert per Mention, or one digest per run?** I recommend **one digest per Daily Check**, grouped by company, with negative Mentions listed first. That's what a portfolio manager wants to read each morning. SpaceX alone could produce 20 Mentions a day, and a separate message for each would bury the one negative story about a small company. If a run finds no new Mentions, it sends no alert and just logs "no new mentions".

**(d) Which channel?** An `AlertNotifier` interface with two implementations, both justified by the brief:
- **Always:** write the digest to the log **and** to `data/alerts/<date>.json`. That's visible with no setup, and it lands in the `data/` folder the reviewers asked for.
- **When `SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL` is set:** post the digest to Slack as well. That's the realistic production channel, and an incoming webhook needs no OAuth app.

Email would need SMTP credentials and is harder for a reviewer to verify, so I'd leave it out.

Do you agree with all four, especially the 7-day staleness guard and digest-per-run? Or do you want something like an immediate alert for negative Mentions?

### Q5: How do we see an alert without waiting until tomorrow?

The fix is to separate *what* the Daily Check does from *when* it runs:

1. **The Daily Check runs on demand.** The scheduler (a cron inside the backend) only calls it. The same code is exposed as a CLI command (`npm run check`) and as an API endpoint (`POST /checks`), and the dashboard gets a "Run check now" button that calls that endpoint.
2. **The Backfill can stop short.** `npm run backfill -- --until=3d` collects the Coverage Window but stops 3 days before today. The next `npm run check` then finds those last 3 days as genuine New Mentions. They're first seen in that run and published within 7 days, so they produce a real Alert Digest straight away. The README's end-to-end steps would be exactly these two commands, followed by "open the dashboard and look at the notification".
3. **An automated test proves the rule.** An end-to-end test runs a Backfill and then a Daily Check against a fake NewsSource and a fake classifier. It asserts that the Backfill produced no digest, the check produced exactly one, a stale Mention was left out, and negative Mentions came first.

Nothing is faked in the real path. Option 2 uses the same code a real day would, and the only difference is where the Backfill stops. The committed `data/` snapshot would come from this exact sequence, so `data/alerts/` contains a real digest.

Does that work for you, the `--until` cutoff in particular? Or would you rather have an explicit demo or seed mode?

### Q6: "No fake data at all": does that include automated tests?

This conflicts with the repo's own rules. `docs/backend-nestjs-instructions.md` requires an in-memory fake for every port, and says end-to-end tests swap it in with `overrideProvider(TOKEN)`. The reason is practical. The CI runner has no Ollama or internet access, and Google News results change every hour, so a test against the live feed can't assert anything stable and can't run in CI at all.

Here is what I think "no fake data" should mean:

| Where | Rule |
|---|---|
| Running app, Backfill and Daily Check, the dashboard, the committed `data/` snapshot | **Real data only.** No seed scripts, no demo mode, no invented companies or articles. |
| Automated tests (CI) | The ports are replaced by in-memory implementations, as the repo standard requires. The articles they return are **recorded from real Google News RSS responses** and committed as fixtures, not invented. The classifier fake returns verdicts we captured from real Ollama runs on those same articles. |
| Optional live test (`npm run test:live`, not in CI) | Hits real Google News and real Ollama for 2–3 companies, to check that the feed format hasn't changed and the model still answers in the expected structure. |

The tests then exercise real-world article shapes (odd titles, non-English snippets, redirect URLs) without depending on the network or a GPU.

Do you mean "no fake data" for the running system only, with tests using recorded real fixtures as above? Or should even the tests run against live services?

### Q7: How is a company's Mention Status defined?

The brief's examples are "3 days ago / 45 days ago / no coverage found". Here is what I propose:

**(a) It counts Mentions only.** Candidates rejected as irrelevant don't count, or Hurricane Harvey would make Harvey look active. Sentiment doesn't matter: a negative Mention is still coverage.

**(b) It doesn't depend on the Coverage Window.** It uses the most recent Mention we've ever stored, whichever window the dashboard is showing. Switching to "Q3 2026" doesn't change it, because it always means "as of now".

**(c) Show the exact age plus a coloured bucket**, so the dashboard can be scanned and sorted:

| Bucket | Last Mention | Meaning for an investor-relations reader |
|---|---|---|
| **Active** | ≤ 7 days | In the news right now |
| **Recent** | 8–30 days | Normal |
| **Quiet** | 31–90 days | Worth a look |
| **No coverage** | none found | Possibly gone silent, renamed or defunct |

**(d) Make "no coverage" precise.** We only ever collect about 90 days back, so we can't tell "never covered" apart from "last covered 8 months ago". The label should say what we actually know: "No coverage found since <earliest date collected>", not "never mentioned". The README states this limitation, and the gap closes over time as the Daily Check builds up history.

Do you agree with the buckets and their thresholds? And with the status ignoring the Coverage Window?

### Q8: Which Ollama model, and how do we call it?

I checked this machine: an Apple M3 with **16 GB of RAM**, and **Ollama isn't installed yet**. Postgres in Docker, the backend and the dashboard will share that memory, so a quantized model of about 7–8B parameters (~5 GB) is the practical ceiling.

| Model | Size (Q4) | Strengths | Weaknesses |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`qwen2.5:7b`** | ~4.7 GB | Very reliable JSON and structured output, strong at following instructions, good multilingual support (Hebrew and German snippets will appear for Israeli and EU companies) | A little slower than 3B models |
| `llama3.1:8b` | ~4.9 GB | Widely known, solid general reasoning | Somewhat less consistent with strict JSON, weaker on non-English text |
| `gemma2:9b` | ~5.4 GB | Good at nuanced sentiment | Tight on 16 GB alongside Docker, slowest of the four |
| `llama3.2:3b` / `qwen2.5:3b` | ~2 GB | 2–3× faster | Noticeably worse at the hard part, judging relevance for ambiguous names |

**My recommendation:** `qwen2.5:7b`, configurable through `OLLAMA_MODEL` so a reviewer with more RAM can swap it. We'd call it like this:
- **`/api/chat` with Ollama's `format` parameter set to a JSON schema**, so the model can only return valid output (`{ relevant: boolean, reason: string }` and `{ sentiment: "positive"|"negative"|"neutral", reason: string }`).
- **`temperature: 0`**, so re-running gives the same verdict and spot checks can be reproduced.
- **A fixed system prompt plus a per-article user message**: the company's name, aliases and description, then the article's title, source, date and snippet. Each prompt includes a few examples of hard cases (a Harvey article that isn't about Harvey the company, and a layoffs story, which is negative even when worded neutrally).
- **Output is checked again on our side.** Anything malformed is a classification failure, retried once, then left unclassified and reported. It is never guessed.

**Validation:** we hand-label about 60 real Candidates, deliberately weighted toward ambiguous names. We report relevance precision and recall and sentiment accuracy in the README, and keep the labelled set as one of the recorded fixtures.

Does `qwen2.5:7b` with these call conventions work for you? (The next question is throughput: a full Backfill could mean many thousands of LLM calls on this laptop.)

### Assistant [2026-10-07T07:34:33.445Z]
I'd run it **outside the container, natively on the host**. On a Mac that's close to a requirement.

**Why:** Docker Desktop on macOS runs containers inside a Linux VM, and that VM **can't use the Apple GPU (Metal)**. Ollama inside Docker on your M3 runs on the CPU only, which for a 7B model is roughly **3–5× slower**. The native macOS Ollama app uses Metal and the unified memory directly. Given the throughput question coming next, that difference matters a lot.

**How it would work:**
- **Ollama runs on the host**, installed with `brew install ollama` (or the macOS app). Then `ollama pull qwen2.5:7b` and `ollama serve`. The README documents exactly these steps.
- **The backend reaches it through `OLLAMA_BASE_URL`**:
  - `http://localhost:11434` when you run `npm run start:dev` natively, which is the default.
  - `http://host.docker.internal:11434` when the backend itself runs in Docker Compose. That gets set in `docker-compose.yml`.
- **The backend fails loudly at startup** if Ollama is unreachable or the model hasn't been pulled. The health check reports it, and the dashboard shows a clear error instead of results that are silently missing.
- **Docker Compose keeps only Postgres**, plus the backend and frontend if you want everything in containers.

**The alternative**, an `ollama` service inside docker-compose, would only pay off on a Linux host with an NVIDIA GPU and the NVIDIA container toolkit. That isn't the reviewers' likely setup or yours. I'd mention it in the README as an option and not build it (YAGNI). The deployed setup on ECS/AWS is a separate concern: the brief only asks for a local run, so I'd keep deployment out of scope.

I'd record this as an ADR, because a future reader will wonder why Ollama is the one dependency missing from `docker-compose.yml`. Shall I? And is it fine to declare cloud deployment out of scope?

Docker Desktop on macOS runs containers in a Linux VM with no access to the Apple GPU (Metal), so a containerised Ollama falls back to CPU and runs a 7B model roughly 3\u20135\u00d7 slower \u2014 decisive when a Backfill means thousands of classification calls. The backend reaches the host's Ollama through `OLLAMA_BASE_URL` (`http://localhost:11434` natively, `http://host.docker.internal:11434` from a container) and refuses to boot if Ollama is unreachable or the configured model is not pulled.\n\n## Considered Options\n\n- **`ollama` service in Docker Compose** \u2014 one fewer install step, but CPU-only on macOS. ### Q9: How should a one- or few-command local run work?

Wrapping it in npm is the right idea. I'd adjust two things about it, explained below.

**Option A: root `package.json` with npm scripts only.** For example, `"start": "ollama serve & docker compose up --build"`.
- ✅ Familiar to a Node reviewer and needs no extra tooling.
- ❌ npm scripts are one-line shell commands. They can't check prerequisites properly, and they behave differently on zsh, bash and Windows `cmd`.
- ❌ `ollama serve &` leaves an orphaned background process. If the Ollama macOS app is already running (it starts at login), `ollama serve` fails because the port is already in use.
- ❌ When something fails, the error is cryptic ("connection refused" from deep in the backend) instead of "Ollama isn't installed, run `brew install ollama`".

**Option B (my recommendation): root `package.json` whose scripts call small Node scripts.**
```
npm run setup    # check Docker, Node ≥22 and Ollama; pull qwen2.5:7b if missing; npm ci in both apps
npm start        # make sure Ollama is serving (start it only if it isn't); docker compose up --build:
                 #   postgres → migrate (one-off) → backend → frontend; print the dashboard URL
npm run backfill -- --until=3d   # optional CLI path; the dashboard has the same control
npm stop         # docker compose down (leaves Ollama alone, since it may be shared)
```
- ✅ Two commands from clone to dashboard. Each failed check prints the exact fix.
- ✅ Written in Node `.mjs` scripts, so it fits the "JavaScript (Node.js)" requirement and works on macOS, Linux and Windows.
- ✅ `npm start` is safe to run repeatedly. It never starts a second Ollama.
- ❌ It's a small amount of extra code (~100 lines) to maintain.
- ❌ It **won't install Ollama itself**. That's a system-level install (brew or a download) that a script shouldn't do without asking. It detects the missing install and prints the command instead.

**Option C: a Makefile or `run.sh`.** Short and conventional, but it doesn't work on Windows and feels out of place in a Node-only brief.

**Option D: Docker Compose plus README steps only.** The most transparent, but it's 6–8 manual commands, and any one of them can be missed. That's exactly what the "set up from the README alone" criterion penalises.

**The two adjustments to your idea:**
1. **Building the frontend happens inside Docker**, in a multi-stage Dockerfile with a Vite build served by a static server. npm doesn't need to build it separately, so `npm start` just runs `docker compose up --build`.
2. **Migrations run as a one-off `migrate` service.** The backend waits for it to finish (`service_completed_successfully`), so a fresh clone gets its schema with no extra command.

For day-to-day development you'd still run `npm run dev`: Postgres in Docker, with the backend and frontend running natively with hot reload.

Option B?

### Assistant [2026-10-07T07:43:01.740Z]
Having NestJS serve the frontend is a reasonable pattern: it would serve the built frontend with `@nestjs/serve-static`. But it works against how this repo is already set up, so I'd keep them separate.

**What it would look like:** the backend image builds the frontend and copies `frontend/dist` in. Nest serves it at `/`, the API moves under `/api`, and every other path falls back to `index.html` so the single-page app's routes work.

**Pros**
- ✅ **One container, one port, one origin.** There's no CORS setup, and the frontend calls the API at `/api/...` with no base-URL config.
- ✅ **Compose is simpler.** There's no frontend service and no reverse proxy.
- ✅ **One artifact to run.** It's a common, respectable pattern for small internal tools like this one.

**Cons**
- ❌ **It breaks the template's build layout.** `ci.yml` builds `docker build ... backend` with `backend/` as the build context. A backend image that contains the frontend needs the repo root as its context, which means changing the CI image job, the backend Dockerfile, and later `deploy.yml`. That fights the scaffold the repo was generated from.
- ❌ **The two services become coupled.** A one-line CSS change rebuilds and restarts the backend image, and the frontend can no longer be built, tested or shipped on its own. That cuts directly against the plan to implement issues in parallel.
- ❌ **It mixes concerns.** The NestJS app takes on static hosting, SPA fallback routing and cache headers, and has to keep them from shadowing `/api/*` and `/health`.
- ❌ **Development doesn't benefit.** In `npm run dev` you'd still run the Vite dev server with hot reload and a proxy, so the single-origin advantage only exists in the Docker run.

**My recommendation:** keep the frontend as its own container, as the template intends, and get the same single origin a different way. The frontend container serves the built files and **proxies `/api` to the backend**. In development, Vite's `server.proxy` does the same job. The browser sees one origin, with no CORS and no base-URL config, CI stays unchanged, and the frontend and backend can be developed in parallel. The cost is one small proxy config, about 10 lines.

I wouldn't write an ADR for this: following the template is the obvious path. Separate frontend container with an `/api` proxy, or do you still prefer Nest serving it?

### User [2026-10-07T07:49:14.480Z]
i want to use session cookie with SameSite=Strict setting  
is it possible with separate frontend container with /api proxy . ?

### Assistant [2026-10-07T07:49:32.924Z]
Yes. The `/api` proxy is what makes it work cleanly.

**Why it works:** the browser only ever talks to the frontend's origin, say `http://localhost:8080`. When the backend replies with `Set-Cookie` through the proxy, the browser stores the cookie for that origin, because it can't tell a proxy is involved. Every later `/api/...` request is same-origin and same-site, so a `SameSite=Strict` cookie is sent. The Vite dev proxy behaves the same way.

**Settings to get right:**
- **Don't set a `Domain` attribute, and use `Path=/`.** The cookie is then scoped to exactly the host the browser sees. If `Domain` were set to the backend's own hostname, the browser would reject the cookie.
- **The proxy must pass `Set-Cookie` through unchanged.** Both nginx and Vite's proxy do this by default. Just don't add cookie-rewriting directives.
- **Use `HttpOnly` always, and `Secure` whenever the site is served over https.** Chrome and Firefox treat `http://localhost` as secure, so `Secure` cookies work there. Older Safari versions don't, so a local run over plain http needs `Secure` controlled by config, with a production default of on.
- **Nest needs `app.set('trust proxy', 1)`.** It then reads `X-Forwarded-Proto` and `X-Forwarded-For` from the proxy and correctly detects https.
- **`SameSite=Strict` mostly closes off cross-site request forgery (CSRF)**, but it isn't a complete defence on its own. I'd also reject state-changing requests (`POST /checks`, Backfill, acknowledging a digest) whose `Origin` header doesn't match. That's one small guard.

(Without a proxy it would still technically work. `localhost:5173` and `localhost:8000` count as the same *site*, because ports don't matter for SameSite. But you'd need CORS with credentials, and that falls apart as soon as the frontend and backend are on different domains. The proxy avoids all of that.)

---

### Q10: What's the session for? Authentication isn't in the brief.

A session cookie implies people log in, and that brings in quite a lot: a user store, password hashing or single sign-on, a login page, logout, session storage and expiry, and protecting every endpoint. The brief doesn't mention access control at all.

| Option | What it means |
|---|---|
| **No auth (my recommendation for this project)** | It's a local tool for a reviewer on `localhost`. Session-based auth goes in the backlog, with your cookie spec (`HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`, same-origin through the proxy) recorded so it's built that way later. |
| **One shared admin login** | Credentials come from env vars, sessions are stored in Postgres, all of `/api` is protected and there's a login page. Adds roughly one issue's worth of work, plus a step for the reviewer. |
| **Real users** | A user table, roles, single sign-on. Clearly out of scope here. |

My reasoning: the evaluation rewards a pipeline that runs end to end and a README a stranger can follow. Every login step is friction for the reviewer, and it protects nothing on `localhost`. But if you want to show security awareness, the shared admin login is a contained way to do it.

Which do you want: no auth for now with the cookie spec in the backlog, a shared admin login now, or something else?

---

### Q11: Can a full Backfill finish on this laptop?

Here's a rough estimate:
- **Candidates:** about 40 heavily covered companies hit Google's cap of ~100 each, and the long tail averages maybe 10–20. That's roughly **8,000–12,000 Candidates**.
- **Cost per call** with `qwen2.5:7b` on an M3: reading the prompt is fast, but generating a JSON verdict with a reason runs at about 20 tokens/second. That's roughly **1.5–2.5 s per call**.
- **Total:** 10,000 relevance calls plus sentiment on the Mentions comes to **about 6–9 hours**. A reviewer can't wait that long, and it makes every iteration during development painful.

I recommend four measures together:

1. **A free check before calling the LLM.** If neither the company's name nor any alias appears in a Candidate's title or snippet, reject it right away with the reason "name not present", and skip the LLM. Google search does return items like that, especially for multi-word names. This doesn't replace the LLM's relevance check; it just avoids wasting calls.
2. **Cap each company at the 50 most recent Candidates per Backfill.** Companies at that level are already sampled because of Google's 100-item cap. The dashboard labels them "50+" so the cap is visible and not mistaken for a full count. The Daily Check isn't capped, because it only sees about a day's worth of items.
3. **Run the Backfill in the background and make it resumable.** It works through one company at a time and saves results as it goes. The dashboard shows progress (for example "112 / 258 companies") and fills in live. If it's interrupted, re-running skips Candidates it has already classified, because deduplication by URL handles that.
4. **Keep LLM output short and allow slight parallelism.** The reason is capped at about 15 words, since output tokens are what cost time, and up to 2 requests are sent to Ollama at once (`OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL`). Both are configurable.

Together I'd expect **roughly 1.5–3 hours** for a full Backfill, and the dashboard becomes useful within minutes. The first implementation issue should **measure** the real per-call time and record it in the README, instead of relying on my estimate.

**One option I'm deliberately not proposing:** skipping the LLM relevance check for companies that look unambiguous. It's tempting (who else is "Quantum Machines"?), but it reopens ADR-002 and adds an "ambiguous" flag someone has to curate correctly.

Do you agree with all four, and with a cap of 50?

### User [2026-10-07T07:56:14.806Z]
explain cap of 50

### Assistant [2026-10-07T07:56:29.616Z]
**What it limits:** only how many Candidates per company the **Backfill** sends to the LLM. The Daily Check isn't capped. The cap only matters for heavily covered companies like SpaceX, Stripe, Anthropic, xAI and Databricks, which would otherwise each eat ~100+ LLM calls. Small companies never reach it.

**Why 50:** around 40 companies hit Google's ~100 limit. Capping them at 50 cuts about 2,000 calls, roughly an hour off the Backfill, while 50 articles is still plenty to judge a company's sentiment mix and recency.

**What it affects:**
| Dashboard element | Effect |
|---|---|
| Mention Status (last mentioned N days ago) | None, as long as the newest articles are among the ones kept |
| Mention count | Becomes a lower bound, so it's shown as "50+" |
| Sentiment split | A sample, not a census. Fine for spotting trends. |
| Mentions over time across the quarter | **This is where my proposal was wrong** |

**What I got wrong.** I said "the 50 most recent". For SpaceX, the 50 most recent articles might all be from the last 3–4 days. The quarterly view would then show SpaceX with no coverage in July and August and a spike this week, which is false and misleading. On top of that, Google News RSS doesn't reliably sort by date, so "most recent" isn't even something we can guarantee from one query.

**Corrected proposal: sample evenly across time.** The Backfill splits the 90-day Coverage Window into **6 slices of 15 days**. It queries each slice separately with Google's `after:` and `before:` operators, and keeps up to **~8 Candidates per slice** (~50 in total).
- The quarterly timeline is honest: a sample from every part of the quarter, not one burst.
- **Coverage is better.** Six queries can reach up to ~600 items where a single 90-day query stops at about 100, and then we sample those down. A small company's 12 articles all get through, because no slice for it ever reaches the cap.
- **The newest slice is always included**, so Mention Status stays accurate.
- It costs 6 lightweight HTTP requests per company instead of 1, which is trivial next to the LLM time.

The dashboard labels a capped company **"Sampled: 50 of 100+ found"**, so a reader knows that company's count and sentiment split are estimates. Both the slice count and the per-slice cap are configurable.

Do you agree with the corrected version: 6 time slices × ~8 per slice, plus the other three measures (the free name check, the resumable background Backfill, and short output with parallelism of 2)?

### Assistant [2026-10-07T07:59:30.535Z]
Yes. In fact the brief asks for exactly that: a `data/` folder "containing the output of a successful run … so we can review results without re-running everything ourselves". A full overnight run on this laptop, committed to the repo, makes the snapshot complete instead of sampled.

That covers the snapshot. There are still three reasons to keep some of the earlier measures:

1. **Reviewers who run it themselves.** The README must let a stranger run the pipeline end to end, and a 6–9 hour wait reads as broken. They need a short path: **`--companies "Quantum Machines,Harvey,Island,..."`** (or `--limit 10`) to backfill a handful of companies in about 15 minutes. That deliberately includes ambiguous names so they can see relevance filtering at work.
2. **Your own iterations.** Every prompt change you want to validate means re-classifying. A cap and a company filter keep development loops down to minutes.
3. **Interruptions.** A 7-hour run on a laptop *will* be interrupted by sleep, a lid close or an Ollama hiccup. Resuming exactly where it stopped matters even more for a long run.

**Revised proposal:**
| Measure | Status |
|---|---|
| Free name check before the LLM | Keep. It only removes noise, so it's free time saved. |
| Resumable background Backfill with progress | Keep. Essential for a long run. |
| Short output, parallelism of 2 | Keep |
| `--companies` / `--limit` filter | **Add.** It's the reviewer's quick path. |
| Per-company cap | **Off by default**, available as `--max-per-company` for development |
| Time slicing (6 × 15 days) | **Your call.** It gives an even timeline and gets past Google's 100-item cap. But for heavily covered companies it *adds* Candidates (SpaceX could produce 300–600), which could push the full run to 12+ hours. Without slicing, heavily covered companies are limited to ~100 items, and those may cluster in time. |

**A related idea:** besides the `data/` JSON export, also commit a **database dump** of that same run, loaded with `npm run restore-snapshot`. A reviewer then sees the full dashboard within a minute of `npm start`, with no Backfill at all. It's real output, so it doesn't break ADR-006's "no fake data" rule. The snapshot does get older as the rolling window moves on, so the dashboard would show the date it's "as of".

There are two decisions here:
- **(a) Time slicing:** yes, for an honest timeline and more coverage at the cost of a longer run? Or no, accepting the ~100-item limit?
- **(b) Database dump plus `restore-snapshot`:** yes or no?

### User [2026-10-07T08:01:16.150Z]
no slicing, why do we need db dump and restore ? cant we restore from the data file ?

### Assistant [2026-10-07T08:01:28.132Z]
Yes, we can, and it's probably the better design. I proposed the dump only because it needs no code. Here's the trade-off:

| | **Database dump** (`pg_dump`) | **Import from `data/` JSON** |
|---|---|---|
| Code to write | None | An exporter that captures everything, plus an importer |
| Completeness | Everything automatically | Only what the export includes, so it must include rejected Candidates, Relevance Verdicts and reasons, first-seen times, Alert Digests and their acknowledged state, and run history |
| Readable in the repo | No, it's an opaque file | Yes. Reviewers can read and diff it. |
| Coupled to the database schema | Yes. It's tied to the Postgres version and migration level, so the next migration can break a restore. | No. It's domain-shaped JSON, so it survives schema changes. |
| Artifacts committed | Two copies of the same run that can drift apart | **One** |

**My recommendation:** import from `data/`. One committed artifact serves as both the reviewable output and the way to load the dashboard. Being independent of the schema is a real advantage, and the "complete export" requirement is something we want anyway: the brief asks how classification quality was validated, and rejected Candidates with their reasons are that evidence.

This **changes ADR-005**, which currently says the export is "never read back". The revised rule:
- Postgres stays the source of truth while the app is running.
- `data/` is a **complete, versioned export** (with a `schemaVersion` field) written after every Backfill and Daily Check.
- **`npm run import-data` loads it into an empty database only.** It refuses to run if the database already has data, so it can't silently merge with or overwrite a live database. Afterwards, the dashboard shows the snapshot's "as of" date.

I'm also recording **no time slicing**: heavily covered companies are limited to Google's ~100 items per query, and those may cluster in time. That goes in ADR-001's consequences and in the README's limitations.

Shall I revise ADR-005 this way?

### Q12: What makes two Candidates "the same article"?

De-duplication decides what counts as one Mention, the "first seen" rule for alerts, and whether a re-run classifies something again. Three real situations:

**(a) One article mentions two Tracked Companies.** *"Anthropic and xAI raise record rounds"* appears in both companies' search results.
**My recommendation:** it's **one article but two Mentions**, one per company, each with its own Relevance Verdict and Sentiment. The verdicts can differ: the article might be positive for Anthropic and negative for xAI. Since Sentiment is defined *toward the company*, a Mention's identity has to be **(article, company)**.

**(b) The same story in several outlets.** A Reuters piece republished by 30 sites, or a press release picked up widely.
**My recommendation:** **count each outlet as a separate Mention.** Investor-relations people do count outlets ("covered in 30 publications"), and every link really is a separate appearance. The dashboard can show the outlet name next to each one. Grouping syndicated copies would need fuzzy title matching, which is a backlog item, not version 1.

**(c) Which URL identifies the article: a real implementation risk.** Google News RSS links look like `news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMi...`. Since 2024 these **can't be resolved by following an HTTP redirect**: the browser follows a JavaScript redirect, and resolving them from a server means calling an undocumented internal Google endpoint, which is fragile and may be rate-limited.
**My recommendation:**
- **Identity key = the Google article ID** (the `CBMi…` token). It's stable for each article and always available.
- **Try to resolve the real publisher URL, best effort.** On success, store it and link to it. On failure, store the Google link, which still opens the article in a browser. Every Mention therefore links back to its source, as the brief requires.
- Store the outlet's name and homepage from the RSS `<source>` element either way.
- The README says this plainly. When a second News Source arrives, de-duplication across sources would need the resolved URL, which is a backlog concern.

Do you agree with all three: Mention = (article, company); syndicated copies counted separately; Google article ID as the identity, with the publisher URL resolved best effort?

### Q13: What does the dashboard show?

This is where "product thinking" gets judged. My proposed layout:

**Top bar:** Coverage Window selector (Last 90 days | Q3 2026 | Q4 2026 to date), an **alert bell** with a count of unacknowledged Alert Digests, and the "as of" timestamp (the last run, or the imported snapshot's date).

**1. Summary strip.** Four numbers that are always shown: companies by Mention Status (Active / Recent / Quiet / No coverage), total Mentions in the window, the overall sentiment split, and **the number of negative Mentions in the window**, which links to a filtered list. That's the number an investor-relations person acts on.

**2. Company table (the main view).** One row per Tracked Company with:
- name
- Mention Status badge and "3 days ago"
- Mentions in the window ("100+" when it hit Google's cap)
- a sentiment split bar (positive / neutral / negative)
- the most recent headline

It can be sorted by any column and filtered by status, by "has negatives", and by name. **Default sort: most negative Mentions in the window first, then by recency**, so problems surface without anyone searching for them. "No coverage" companies sink to the bottom, but a single click filters to them.

**3. Company detail (a side panel or its own page):**
- a weekly Mentions chart across the window, stacked by sentiment
- the Mention list: date, Outlet, headline linked to the original, sentiment badge, and the classifier's one-line reason
- the Company Profile in use (aliases, description, search terms), so a reviewer can see *why* something was judged relevant
- a toggle to **show rejected Candidates** with their reasons. That's the auditing view, and it backs up the quality claims.

**4. Alert Digest panel** (opened from the bell): each digest lists New Mentions grouped by company with negatives first, and has an "Acknowledge" button.

**5. Operations panel:** Backfill control (cutoff, company filter) with live progress ("112 / 258 companies, 3,410 Candidates classified"), "Run check now", the last few runs with their status and counts, and Ollama health (reachable, model pulled).

**Technical choices:** Recharts for the charts. The frontend **polls** for run progress every few seconds, which is simpler than server-sent events and good enough for a single user.

Do you agree with the layout, and especially the default sort (negatives first)? Is anything missing or unnecessary?

### Q14: How do runs behave: scheduling, overlapping runs and failures?

A Backfill and a Daily Check are both a **Run**. Here's what I propose for how they behave:

**(a) Scheduling.** A cron inside the backend (`@nestjs/schedule`), **daily at 07:00 Asia/Jerusalem**, configurable through `DAILY_CHECK_CRON` and `TZ`, so the digest is waiting at the start of the day.
- **Catching up:** a laptop is often asleep at 07:00. When the backend starts, if the last successful Daily Check is more than 24 hours old, it runs one immediately.
- **"Since the previous run"** = from the start of the last *successful* Daily Check minus 1 day of overlap. The overlap costs nothing, because duplicates are dropped and already-classified Articles aren't classified again, and it catches Articles Google surfaced late.

**(b) Only one Run at a time.** A Backfill and a Daily Check never overlap. They'd duplicate work and compete for Ollama. A lock in the database (at most one Run with status `running`) enforces this, and starting another returns **409 Conflict** with "Backfill in progress, 112/258". The dashboard greys out the buttons while a Run is going.

**(c) Failures.**
- **Fetching fails for one company:** record the error against that company and **carry on** with the rest. The Run ends as `completed_with_errors` and the operations panel lists which companies failed.
- **Ollama fails:** after **5 consecutive** classification failures, Ollama is treated as down and the Run stops as `failed`. Fetched Candidates are **stored unclassified** and picked up by the next Run, because classification is a separate step that can be retried.
- **Crash or restart mid-Run:** on startup, any Run still marked `running` is changed to `interrupted`. Starting again resumes it, because work already done is skipped.

**(d) One correction to "New Mention".** Take an Article fetched in Monday's run that couldn't be classified because Ollama was down, then classified as relevant on Tuesday. Under "first seen" it would never alert. The definition becomes **"first *confirmed as a Mention* in this Daily Check, and published within the last 7 days"**. Stated that way, failures can't swallow alerts.

Do you agree with all four, including 07:00 Asia/Jerusalem, the 5-failure threshold, and the corrected New Mention definition?

### Q15: Should a minimal in-app schedule stay, for the brief's sake?

One risk before we drop scheduling entirely. The brief's third goal is literally *"Implement a **daily job** that checks for new press mentions"*. The architecture section asks for *"a scheduled job (cron, script, or workflow tool)"*, and correctness is judged on "the three required outputs". With only a button and EventBridge out of scope, a reviewer could reasonably mark goal 3 as half done: there's an alert, but nothing runs daily.

| Option | What it is | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| **Tiny in-app cron (my recommendation)** | `@nestjs/schedule` calls the same Run trigger daily at a time set by `DAILY_CHECK_CRON`. It's switched on by `DAILY_CHECK_SCHEDULE_ENABLED`, which defaults to on locally and gets turned off in production when EventBridge takes over. No catch-up logic and no extras. | About 15 lines and one config entry. It respects the one-Run lock, so if a Run is already going it just logs and skips. |
| **Button only** | Matches what you said exactly | Goal 3 can be challenged, and the README has to argue that EventBridge is the scheduler even though nothing about it is built |
| **Document a host crontab line** | The README says to add a `curl -X POST localhost:8080/api/checks` crontab entry | No code, but the reviewer has to set it up by hand, and it's easy to skip |

The in-app cron doesn't conflict with EventBridge later. Both call the same entry point, and the flag decides which one is active.

Keep the tiny flag-controlled cron, or go button-only?

### Q16: How do we separate the collector from the API server?

**Proposal: one backend codebase with two entry points, built into the same image and run as separate processes.**

```
backend/src/
  main.ts            → API server: HTTP only. Never fetches news or calls Ollama.
  worker.ts          → Collector: a Nest app with no HTTP. Runs Backfills and Daily Checks, owns the cron.
  coverage/ runs/ alerts/ ...   shared domain modules; each entry point imports only what it needs
```
- In `docker-compose.yml` the same image is used twice: `api` runs `node dist/main.js`, and `collector` runs `node dist/worker.js`.
- The API module graph **never imports** the NewsSource or classifier modules. A lint rule enforces that boundary, so the separation can't quietly erode.

**How they communicate: through Postgres, using the Runs table as the queue.**
1. The dashboard calls `POST /api/runs {type: "backfill", until, companies}`. The API **inserts a Run with status `queued`** and returns 202 Accepted. The one-Run rule becomes "at most one Run that is `queued` or `running`", enforced by a partial unique index, and a conflict returns 409.
2. The collector checks for queued Runs every few seconds. It claims one with `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, runs it, and writes progress to the Run row.
3. The dashboard keeps polling the API, which reads progress from that row.
4. The cron inside the collector does the same thing: it inserts a queued Daily Check.

**Why this approach:**
- **No new infrastructure.** We already need a Runs table for the lock, progress and history, so making it the queue costs almost nothing. The repo rules say not to add a service CI doesn't need. Redis with BullMQ would mean a Redis container plus CI changes for a queue that only ever holds one job.
- **Each process fails on its own.** If Ollama is down, the collector fails while the API keeps serving the dashboard. That also refines ADR-007: **only the collector** refuses to start without Ollama. The collector writes a **heartbeat** row with its Ollama health, and the dashboard shows "Collector offline" or "Ollama unreachable" instead of an API error.
- **The production path is clear.** The collector becomes its own ECS service, or EventBridge can launch it as an ECS task (`node dist/worker.js --run=check`). If a real queue (SQS) is ever needed, enqueuing sits behind a `RunQueue` port, so only that adapter changes.
- **CI doesn't change.** It's still `backend/`, with one image.

**Alternatives I rejected:**
- **A separate `collector/` package** with its own `package.json`: domain types and entities would be duplicated or need a shared package, and `ci.yml` and the Dockerfiles would grow a third service.
- **pg-boss** (a Postgres-backed job queue library): a reasonable choice, but it's a library and its own schema for something a single table we need anyway already covers.
- **Redis with BullMQ:** extra infrastructure, as explained above.

Do you agree: two entry points, one image, Runs table as the queue with `SKIP LOCKED`, and a heartbeat for collector health? If so, I'll record it as an ADR, because it's architectural and someone will eventually suggest "just use BullMQ".

### User [2026-10-07T08:28:33.128Z]
no 2 processes on same container  sound like a hack rather production ready setup.

### Assistant [2026-10-07T08:28:42.391Z]
I think my wording misled you. I'm not proposing two processes in one container. I agree that would be a hack: you'd need a process supervisor, a crash in one is hidden by the other, and they can't be scaled or restarted independently.

What I proposed is **two separate containers, each running one process**, which are built from the same image:

```yaml
# docker-compose.yml
services:
  api:
    build: ./backend
    command: ["node", "dist/main.js"]     # one process: the HTTP API
    ports: ["8000"]
  collector:
    build: ./backend
    command: ["node", "dist/worker.js"]   # one process: the collector, no ports
```

Each runs as its own container in Compose, and later as its own ECS service or task, with its own restarts, logs, health, scaling and resource limits. A collector crash or Ollama outage leaves the API untouched. Using one image for several roles is the standard production pattern (web + Sidekiq in Rails, Django + Celery, API + worker in Nest/Bull). It keeps both on the same code version, so they never disagree about the database schema.

If what you mean is that they should also be **separate images**, that can be done without splitting the codebase. The Dockerfile gets two targets:

```dockerfile
FROM builder AS api
CMD ["node", "dist/main.js"]

FROM builder AS collector
CMD ["node", "dist/worker.js"]
```
In Compose that's `build: { context: ./backend, target: api }` for one and `target: collector` for the other. The trade-off is two images to build, tag and push in CI and deploys, for little gain: the dependencies are almost identical, and the collector's are a superset (the RSS parser and the Ollama client).

**My recommendation:** two containers from one image, one process each. Is that what you want, or do you want separate image targets too?

The API never fetches news or calls Ollama \u2014 its module graph does not import the News Source or classifier modules, and a lint rule enforces that boundary. They coordinate only through Postgres: the API inserts a Run with status `queued` (returning 202, or 409 while another Run is queued or running, enforced by a partial unique index); the collector claims it with `SELECT \u2026 FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, writes progress to the Run row, and records a heartbeat with its Ollama health that the dashboard displays.\n\n## Considered Options\n\n- **Two processes in one container** \u2014 rejected: needs a supervisor, hides one process's crash behind the other, and prevents independent restart and scaling.\n- **Separate image per role** (Dockerfile targets) \u2014 two images to build and push for near-identical dependencies; one image also guarantees both roles run the same code against the same schema.\n- **Separate `collector/` package** \u2014 duplicates domain types and entities or forces a shared package, and adds a third service to CI.\n- **Redis + BullMQ** \u2014 a new service in docker-compose and CI for a queue that holds at most one job.\n- **pg-boss** \u2014 a reasonable Postgres-backed queue, but a library and its own schema for what the Runs table, needed anyway for the lock, progress and history, already provides.\n\n## Consequences\n\n- Only the collector requires Ollama at boot (refines ADR-007); the API and dashboard stay up when Ollama is down and report it from the heartbeat.\n- Enqueueing sits behind a `RunQueue` port, so moving to SQS or an EventBridge-launched ECS task later replaces one adapter.\n- A Run is picked up within the collector's polling interval (a few seconds), not instantly.\n"})

### Q17: Who writes `companies.json`, and how much of it?

Writing 258 profiles by hand is a lot of work. I'd scope it down:

1. **Derived automatically from the seed list, with no curation:** the 12 "formerly X" entries are parsed into `name` + `aliases` (`Lifeward` + `ReWalk`), and the 2 domain hints become `domain` (`Lambda` → `lambda.ai`). That parser runs at startup, so the seed list stays the source of truth for names.
2. **Curated: only the ambiguous names, about 40–50 of them.** These need `searchTerms` and a `description`: Harvey, Wave, Ro, Peak, Near, Shield, Guild, Astra, Silo, Orchard, Launchpad, Casper, Tala, Bites, Island, Overtime, Kini, Lano, Ukko, Glean, Groq, Lambda, Neura, Rewire, Clinch, Sense Education, MST, Ride Vision and so on.
3. **Everyone else gets no entry.** They're searched by exact quoted name, and the relevance check gets just the name. If the first Backfill shows a company with a high rejection rate, that's the signal to add a profile for it. The operations panel can list "companies with the most rejected Candidates" to make that easy to spot.

**Who writes the curated part:** I draft the ~45 entries as one issue, using web lookups (each company's real domain and one line about what it does). **You review them in the PR.** That's different from the "LLM-generated descriptions" ADR-003 rejected. That option meant Ollama generating descriptions automatically at runtime with no review. This is a one-time, human-reviewed, versioned file with a source cited for each entry. I'd add one sentence to ADR-003 making that distinction explicit.

**One guard:** any profile field that names the company must match a seed-list entry. That's already the rule, and startup fails otherwise.

Do you agree with the scope (automatic parsing, about 45 curated entries, the rest bare) and with me drafting them for your review?

Here's the full structure, with how each field is used.

**Two files, two jobs:**
- `docs/ourcrowd_companies.txt` (unchanged) is the **source of truth for which companies are tracked**. One name per line, exactly as OurCrowd supplied it.
- `backend/config/companies.json` (new, optional per company) holds the **Company Profiles**: extra context *only* where a name alone isn't enough to find the right articles.

**`companies.json` shape:** an object keyed by the exact seed-list line.

```jsonc
{
  "schemaVersion": 1,
  "profiles": {
    "Harvey": {
      "description": "AI platform for legal and professional services firms",
      "domain": "harvey.ai",
      "aliases": ["Harvey AI"],
      "searchTerms": ["\"Harvey AI\"", "\"Harvey\" legal AI"],
      "source": "https://www.harvey.ai/company"
    },
    "Island": {
      "description": "Enterprise browser for security and IT control",
      "domain": "island.io",
      "searchTerms": ["\"Island\" enterprise browser"],
      "source": "https://www.island.io/about"
    },
    "Lifeward (formerly known as ReWalk)": {
      "description": "Medical exoskeletons and rehabilitation technology",
      "domain": "golifeward.com"
      // aliases ["Lifeward", "ReWalk"] are NOT written here: they're parsed from the seed line
    }
  }
}
```

**What each field does:**

| Field | Used by | Purpose | If missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| *(key)* | Validation | Must match a seed-list line exactly, or **startup fails** | n/a |
| `description` | **RelevanceClassifier prompt** | One line telling the LLM which "Harvey" we mean | The prompt gets the name only |
| `domain` | Relevance prompt; possibly the publisher-URL check later | An extra identity clue ("harvey.ai") | Omitted |
| `aliases` | **Search** (each alias is also queried) **and the free name check before the LLM** | Other names the press uses | Just the display name |
| `searchTerms` | **Search only**: replaces the default query | Narrows Google results for common-word names | Default query: `"<name>"` plus each alias in quotes |
| `source` | Humans reviewing the file | Where the description was checked | n/a, but required whenever `description` is present |

**What's derived automatically from the seed line, so nobody writes it:**
- `Lifeward (formerly known as ReWalk)` → display name `Lifeward`, alias `ReWalk`
- `Lambda (lambda.ai)` → display name `Lambda`, domain `lambda.ai`
- `SSI (Safe Superintelligence)` → display name `SSI`, alias `Safe Superintelligence`
- `One Zero Digital Bank Ltd.` → plus alias `One Zero Digital Bank` (legal suffixes are stripped)

**What a company with no entry gets** (most of the 258): search for `"Quantum Machines"`, the free name check looks for "Quantum Machines", and the relevance prompt gets only the name. That's enough when a name is distinctive.

**Where I'm unsure myself, and what you might be reacting to:**
1. **The file is keyed by the raw seed line** (`"Lifeward (formerly known as ReWalk)"`). That's awkward, but it guarantees a match with the source of truth. The alternative is a generated slug (`lifeward`), which is cleaner to type but adds a mapping step that can drift.
2. **It's optional per company**, so some companies have context and others don't, and the classifier sees inconsistent input. The alternative is **an entry for all 258** (description required for everyone). That's more consistent, but it means 258 entries to research and review, and many defunct companies (Magisto, Nanorep) are hard to describe accurately.

Which part are you unsure about: the structure itself, optional versus full coverage, the raw-line keys, or who writes it?

### Assistant [2026-10-07T09:06:39.620Z]
That's a sound call, and it pulls the backlog item "Company Profile curation API" into version 1. Before I rewrite ADR-003, there are four details that change with it. My recommendation for each:

**(a) Where companies come from, and whether the seed file stays the source of truth.**
- `ourcrowd_companies.txt` is synced into a `tracked_companies` table **at collector and API startup**. That way it works with `npm start` and with `import-data`.
- **The source name is read-only.** The page can't add, rename or delete companies; the file controls that.
- **A company added to the file** appears with an empty profile. **A company removed from the file** is marked `untracked`, not deleted. Its history stays and it drops off the dashboard.
- The fields parsed from the seed line (display name, "formerly" alias, domain hint) are **pre-filled and editable**. The raw source line itself never changes.

**(b) How the first curated profiles get in.** With the JSON file gone, the ~45 ambiguous names need their search terms and descriptions from somewhere.
- **Option 1 (my recommendation):** I draft them once, with a `source` link for each, as `backend/seed/company-profiles.json`. A seeding step applies an entry **only to a profile that's still empty**, so it never overwrites an edit made in the UI. You review that file in its PR, and after that the page is the only place profiles get edited. It's real curated data, so ADR-006 isn't broken.
- **Option 2:** no seed. You type them into the page before the first Backfill. That's the cleanest model, but it's ~45 manual entries before anything works, and a fresh-clone reviewer who skips the page gets poor relevance for Harvey, Island and the rest.

**(c) What an edit affects.** Changing search terms or a description applies to **future Runs only**. Existing Relevance Verdicts and Sentiments stay as they are, because re-classifying history silently would make the dashboard change under people. Each Mention records which **profile version** classified it, so the audit view can say "classified with profile v2". A **"re-classify this company"** button is a backlog item. Edits are allowed during a Run, and the Run uses whatever profile it read when it reached that company.

**(d) The editing page.**
- A table of companies, each opening a form: source name (read-only), display name, description, domain, aliases (chips), search terms (chips), source link, and last updated.
- **Validation is done server-side with DTOs**, for example: search terms must not be empty strings, the domain must be a valid hostname, and a description requires a `source` link.
- Includes a "**Preview search**" button that runs the search terms against Google News and shows the first 10 results *without* classifying or saving anything, so you can tune terms before a Backfill. It goes through the API, which then has to call the NewsSource. **This breaks ADR-009's "the API never fetches news"** unless the preview is done as a small queued job for the collector. I'd put the preview in the backlog and keep the boundary clean.
- No auth, consistent with the backlog: anyone who can reach localhost can edit.

Do you agree with (a)–(d): file sync with source name read-only, a one-time seed that only fills empty profiles, edits affecting future Runs only with the profile version recorded, and "Preview search" moved to the backlog?

I've taken all of that on board. I've marked ADR-003 as superseded and removed the curation API from the backlog, since it's now in scope. "Preview search" went into the backlog. Here's how I read your decisions, before I write the new ADR. I've added two gaps your answer leaves open.

**My reading:**
- **The database is the source of truth for Tracked Companies.** `ourcrowd_companies.txt` is a starter, imported once into an empty table.
- **The source name** (the raw seed line) is the only read-only field. Everything else is editable, including the display name. Companies added on the page have an **empty source name**, which marks them as not from OurCrowd's list.
- **No curated seed file and no file review.** All review happens on the company list page.
- **Companies that are ambiguous at all start as "Needs review" and get no news collected** until someone reviews them on the page. Unambiguous ones are collected straight away.
- **Re-processing replaces history.** No profile versions are kept on Mentions.
- **(d) stays as proposed.**

---

### Q18a: Who decides that a company is ambiguous at import?

Something has to set "Needs review" on Harvey but not on Quantum Machines, and there's no curated file any more.

| Option | How it decides | Weakness |
|---|---|---|
| **Rules only** | Flag it if the name (ignoring a "formerly" part) is a single common English word or a person's first name (from a bundled word list), or is 3 characters or fewer (Ro, MST, Kini) | Misses names that are rare words but common in news ("Groq"? "Glean"? "Lemonade"), and flags harmless ones |
| **Ollama triage once at import (my recommendation)** | The collector asks the model one question per company: "Would a news search for this exact name return many articles about unrelated things?" It returns `{ ambiguous, reason }`, **flagging on any doubt**. The rules above are applied too: **a company flagged by either goes to Needs review.** | 258 extra LLM calls once (~10 minutes). The LLM could miss one, but that's caught later (see below). |
| **Probe search** | Search the bare name, classify 10 results, and flag it if more than 30% are irrelevant | Most accurate, but ~2,500 LLM calls before anything starts |

This is consistent with ADR-003's rejected option. That rejection was about the LLM **inventing descriptions** that steer decisions silently. Here the LLM only raises a **flag that a person then resolves**, and it fails safe: a wrong flag costs a review, it doesn't produce wrong data.

**Safety net:** the dashboard surfaces companies whose Candidates have a **high relevance rejection rate**, which is the signal that an "unambiguous" company was actually ambiguous. One click moves it to Needs review.

### Q18b: Removing a company, and what "re-classify" covers

- **Remove:** I'd recommend **deactivating, not deleting**. A deactivated company is excluded from Runs and the dashboard, and its Mentions are kept. Hard delete with history would be a separate, explicit action. Or do you want plain delete?
- **Re-process:** editing search terms means the existing Candidates came from the *old* query, so re-classifying them isn't enough. I'd make it **"Re-process company"**: delete that company's Candidates and Mentions, then queue a Backfill limited to that one company. That's a fresh search and fresh classification, it reuses existing code, and **it never alerts**, because it's a Backfill. Saving a review on a "Needs review" company offers the same action. Does that match what you meant by "remove history after re-classify"?

One implementation detail for the plan: `import-data` has to insert the snapshot's ids explicitly and then advance the sequence, so later inserts don't collide.

**I read "give a new id" as:** re-adding a deactivated company creates a new Tracked Company with a new id, and doesn't revive the old one. That's what the ADR says, so tell me if you meant something else.

### Q19: Which languages and regions do we search?

Google News RSS returns a regional edition, chosen by `hl`, `gl` and `ceid`. Many of these companies are Israeli, so a lot of real coverage is in **Hebrew** (Globes, Calcalist, TheMarker) as well as English-language Israeli outlets (Globes English, Times of Israel, CTech).

| Option | Coverage | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| **English, US edition only** | International and US tech press, plus English-language Israeli outlets | Misses Hebrew-only coverage |
| **English (US) + Hebrew (Israel) editions (my recommendation)** | Adds Hebrew press, where much of the coverage of smaller Israeli companies appears | 2 queries per company. More Candidates to classify. Hebrew snippets for the LLM, which `qwen2.5:7b` handles reasonably well. Headlines on the dashboard are mixed-language. |
| English, several editions (US, UK, IL) | Mostly overlapping results | Duplicates with different Google article IDs, since the same article appears under each edition |

Under my recommendation, the editions searched are **configurable** (`NEWS_EDITIONS=en-US,he-IL`). Each Mention records its language and the dashboard shows a small language tag. Sentiment is always judged toward the company, whatever the article's language. The relevance and sentiment prompts state that input may be Hebrew and the output is always English JSON. The hand-labelled validation set includes about 10 Hebrew items, so the README can say how well Hebrew is handled.

**A trade-off to watch:** a Hebrew article and its English translation are different Articles at different Outlets, so both count, just like syndicated copies (ADR-008).

English and Hebrew, English only, or something else?

### Q20: How do we capture "the full prompt used with AI coding assistants"?

That's deliverable 6, and it's easy to lose because it accumulates across many sessions. I found `.claude/export-before-compact.py`, a hook that renders a session transcript to plain text before compaction. It appends to `compact-transcript-export.log`, which is **gitignored**, so right now nothing reaches the repo. And once implementation fans out, each subagent runs its own session with its own transcript.

**My recommendation:**
- **`docs/ai-prompts/` committed to the repo**, with one file per session: `2026-10-07-design-grill.md`, `issue-12-news-source.md`, and so on. Each contains the user prompts and the assistant replies, without tool output (file dumps and command output would be huge and add nothing).
- **A small export script** (`scripts/export-ai-prompts`) that reuses the hook's rendering logic. It picks this project's sessions from `~/.claude/projects/...`, including subagent sessions, and writes them to `docs/ai-prompts/`. You run it before submitting, and again at the end of each milestone.
- **Plus an index file `docs/ai-prompts/README.md`** listing the sessions in order, with the prompt for each subagent highlighted (the issue body plus the skill instructions), so a reviewer can follow how the work was directed.
- **Secrets check:** the script redacts anything that looks like a token or key before writing. This session has none, but implementation sessions might touch `.env` files.

The script is Python, like the existing hook. It's repo tooling, not part of the solution, so the brief's Node.js requirement doesn't apply. I'd still list it in the plan.

Agreed? And does that close the design? I think the remaining questions (exact endpoints, schema, prompt wording) belong in the implementation plan, not here. If you agree, I'll write the plan file and create the milestones and issues next.

### Assistant [2026-10-07T09:28:50.419Z]
I ran the hook on a copy of the script, against this session's real transcript. The script works: it exits cleanly and renders all 79 user turns and 79 assistant turns, about 144 KB. But as currently set up, **the export would never reach your submission**:

1. **Its output is gitignored.** It writes to `compact-transcript-export.log` at the repo root, and that file is in `.gitignore`, so it would never be committed.
2. **It appends a full copy every time.** The transcript file keeps the whole session even after compaction, so every compaction plus the session end appends *another complete copy*. A long implementation session would produce the same conversation 3–5 times over in one file.
3. **The hooks are registered only in `.claude/settings.local.json`.** Your global git ignore excludes that file, so it's never committed and **doesn't exist in new worktrees**. Any session you start inside a `.claude/worktrees/...` directory wouldn't export anything.
4. **A cosmetic bug:** a session-end export is labelled `PreCompact export | trigger=unknown`.

**Proposed fix (small):**
- **Write one file per session to `docs/ai-prompts/<date>-<session-id>.md`, overwriting it each time.** Each compaction or session end then rewrites the complete, up-to-date transcript, with no duplicates, in a committed folder.
- **Always write into the primary checkout**, found with `git rev-parse --git-common-dir`, even when the session runs in a worktree. Every transcript then ends up in one place on `main`.
- **Move the two hook registrations to a committed `.claude/settings.json`** and remove them from `settings.local.json`, so they apply in every checkout and worktree.
- Fix the header so it shows the actual event (PreCompact or SessionEnd) and its reason.

**The one remaining manual step is committing.** A hook can write the file, but it can't safely commit to `main` on its own. To remove that step too, I'd add one line to `CLAUDE.md`: *"When committing docs, include any new or updated files in `docs/ai-prompts/`."* Then any later docs commit, including the plan commit I'm about to make, carries them along without you having to remember. This is a docs-and-tooling change, so it goes on a short branch with a PR, per CLAUDE.md.

Shall I make this fix? Once it's done the design is closed, and I'll move on to the plan file, milestones and issues.

I fixed the transcript export hook and opened **PR #1**: https://github.com/yosefsha/ourcrowd_press_cov/pull/1

- **Where transcripts go:** the hook now overwrites one complete transcript per session at `docs/ai-prompts/<date>-<session-id>.md`, in the primary checkout. Sessions run from a worktree write there too.
- **Hook registration:** both hooks now live in a committed `.claude/settings.json`, so they work in every checkout and worktree, not just this machine's local settings.
- **Label:** the header now shows the real event and its reason.
- **`CLAUDE.md`:** a new section says docs commits should include `docs/ai-prompts/`, which removes the commit step you'd otherwise have to remember.
- **`.gitignore`:** I removed the line for the old log file.

I tested it by running the hook from the worktree against this session's real transcript, once as a session end and once as a compaction. Both exited cleanly and left one file, overwritten rather than duplicated, with the right label. That run already created `docs/ai-prompts/2026-10-07-1a15e264….md` in the primary checkout. Until the PR is merged, `main` still runs the old script, so that file won't refresh by itself before then.

**What's waiting on you:**
- **Merge PR #1.** I haven't merged it; per CLAUDE.md that needs you to name the PR. After merging, delete the duplicate hook entries from your local `.claude/settings.local.json`. Otherwise the hook runs twice, which is harmless but redundant.
- **The design docs are still uncommitted in the primary checkout:** `CONTEXT.md`, `docs/adr/ADR-001`–`ADR-010`, `docs/BACKLOG.md` and `docs/ourcrowd_companies.txt`. Two other changes aren't mine and I left them alone: `docs/TASKS.md` was already modified, and `plan_grill.txt` is from your `/export`.

The design is closed. Say the word and I'll write the implementation plan file, commit it with the design docs (docs-only, straight to `main`), and create the GitHub milestones and issues, split along the interfaces so they can be built in parallel.
